[Director 指令] [Patch] exp-019 Δθ 诊断表 — 新增 Appendix Table

**新数据**: exp-019 后处理完成。MFRM-only vs G-MFRM 的 per-model θ 差异与 c_n 完美秩相关（Spearman ρ = 1.0）。

**添加位置**: Appendix 新增一个 section "Contamination Bias Absorption"，放在 Per-Model Estimates (app:raw_scores) 之后。

**表格内容**:

| Model | θ_MFRM | θ_GMFRM | Δθ | c_n |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| gpt-3.5-turbo | -2.487 | -3.274 | +0.786 | 5.587 |
| gpt-4-0613 | -1.446 | -1.877 | +0.431 | 4.168 |
| qwen2.5-72b | 1.213 | 1.178 | +0.035 | 2.961 |
| llama-3.3-70b | 0.518 | 0.539 | -0.022 | 2.478 |
| gemma-2-9b | -0.661 | -0.500 | -0.161 | 1.654 |
| qwen3-8b | 0.208 | 0.513 | -0.305 | 1.178 |
| gpt-4o-mini | 1.064 | 1.432 | -0.368 | 0.934 |
| gpt-4o | 1.591 | 1.988 | -0.397 | 0.880 |

**表格 caption**: "Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$) between $\Delta\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\ref{sec:confounding}."

**正文引用**: 在 §4.2 的 "sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates" 句子后，加一句引用这个表："A per-model diagnostic (Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (gpt-3.5-turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (gpt-4o)---precisely the pattern predicted by the OVB analysis."

**注意页面**: 这是 Appendix 新增内容，不影响 8 页正文限制。但正文引用加了一句话，确保不溢出。

302	\section{Per-Model Estimates}
303	\label{app:raw_scores}

All seven baselines produce identical rankings (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), with ability scores differing by $< 0.03$ logits across methods; G-MFRM is the only method that alters the ranking, swapping gpt-4o-mini and Qwen2.5-72B at ranks 2--3.

315	GPT-4o & $+2.00$ [$1.70$, $2.30$] & $0.88$ [$0.05$, $2.20$] & 1 & 1 \\

325	\caption{G-MFRM posterior estimates for ability ($\hat{\theta}$) and contamination susceptibility ($\hat{c}_n$) with approximate 95\% credible intervals (empirical coverage ${\sim}$71\%; see Limitations). _source line 3579 · 2026-05-23T09:15:22.148Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01R9jK6HNdfequFfCkwA56vv`</summary>

```text
50	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
51	
```

  "new_string": "\\label{tab:raw_scores}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Contamination Bias Absorption}\n\\label{app:bias_absorption}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption} compares per-model ability estimates from MFRM-only and G-MFRM. The difference $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}}$ & $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\theta$ & $\\hat{c}_n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-3.5-Turbo & $-2.487$ & $-3.274$ & $+0.786$ & $5.587$ \\\\\nGPT-4-0613 & $-1.446$ & $-1.877$ & $+0.431$ & $4.168$ \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B & $+1.213$ & $+1.178$ & $+0.035$ & $2.961$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.3-70B & $+0.518$ & $+0.539$ & $-0.022$ & $2.478$ \\\\\nGemma-2-9B & $-0.661$ & $-0.500$ & $-0.161$ & $1.654$ \\\\\nQwen3-8B & $+0.208$ & $+0.513$ & $-0.305$ & $1.178$ \\\\\nGPT-4o-mini & $+1.064$ & $+1.432$ & $-0.368$ & $0.934$ \\\\\nGPT-4o & $+1.591$ & $+1.988$ & $-0.397$ & $0.880$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) between $\\Delta\\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}.}\n\\label{tab:bias_absorption}\n\\end{table}"
}

The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$) between $\\Delta\\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}.}",
          "+\\label{tab:bias_absorption}",
          "+\\end{table}"
        ]
      }

These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. _source line 3615 · 2026-05-23T09:20:52.745Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] exp-015 完整结果 + c_n coverage framing — 2 项修改

### 1. exp-015 Synthetic Calibration (20 seeds) — 新增 Appendix + 正文引用

**新增 Appendix section**: "Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale" (app:synthetic_calibration)

表格内容（汇总统计）:

| Metric | Sequential | Joint G-MFRM | Improvement |
|--------|-----------|-------------|-------------|
| θ RMSE | 0.166 ± 0.049 | 0.083 ± 0.024 | −50.1% |
| θ τ | 0.882 ± 0.117 | 0.954 ± 0.046 | +7.2pp |
| c RMSE | 1.349 | 0.503 | −62.7% |

**Appendix 正文**: "We generate synthetic data at matched scale (N=8 models, I=176 items, J=9 judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: θ-RMSE is halved (0.083 vs.\ 0.166), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean τ = 0.954 vs.\ 0.882). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank p < 0.002). The synthetic effect size (Δτ = +0.072) closely matches the observed real-data effect (Δτ = +0.071). All 40 MCMC fits converge (R̂ = 1.0, ESS ≥ 1678)."

**§4.2 正文引用**: 在 ranking improvement 段落（"Ranking improvement (Δτ = +0.071) is not statistically significant at N = 8..."）后加一句:
"However, synthetic replication at matched scale confirms this effect is consistent and statistically significant across 20 independent seeds (Wilcoxon p < 0.002; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}), suggesting that real-data non-significance reflects single-dataset bootstrap conservatism rather than method failure."

### 2. c_n Coverage Framing — Limitations 更新

当前 Limitations 第四点说 "empirical CI coverage is 71%"。改为区分 θ 和 c_n:

替换为: "Fourth, empirical CI coverage differs by parameter type: ability estimates θ approach nominal coverage at moderate sample sizes (~90\% at N=8), while contamination susceptibility c_n exhibits moderate posterior overconfidence (71\% at N=50, 58\% at N=8). The c_n under-coverage reflects the multiplicative coupling c_n \cdot e_i and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at c_n = 0, which systematically narrows posterior credible intervals. Point estimates and rank orderings (τ = 0.857) are unaffected; c_n estimates are best interpreted as directional diagnostics rather than calibrated intervals."

**注意**: 更新 "five limitations" → 确认计数仍为 "six limitations"（之前已加 rank-1 第六点）。第四点内容改了但数量不变。

**编译后确认 8 页正文。**

333	Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption} compares per-model ability estimates from MFRM-only and G-MFRM. The difference $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.

348	Qwen3-8B & $+0.208$ & $+0.513$ & $-0.305$ & $1.178$ \\
349	GPT-4o-mini & $+1.064$ & $+1.432$ & $-0.368$ & $0.934$ \\
350	GPT-4o & $+1.591$ & $+1.988$ & $-0.397$ & $0.880$ \\
351	\bottomrule
352	\end{tabular}
353	\caption{Contamination bias absorption in MFRM-only estimation. $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$. Models with high contamination susceptibility ($c_n$) have inflated ability estimates under MFRM-only; models with low $c_n$ are relatively deflated. The perfect rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$) between $\Delta\theta$ and $c_n$ empirically confirms the omitted variable bias predicted in Section~\ref{sec:confounding}.}
354	\label{tab:bias_absorption}
355	\end{table}

```text
1	We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\Delta\tau = 0.071$ at $\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \approx 17$ models for 80\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, posterior CIs exhibit moderate overconfidence (empirical coverage 71\% vs.\ 95\% nominal; 50 Monte Carlo seeds; \citealp{talts2018sbc}) due to the HalfNormal boundary constraint on $c_n$, a known limitation of constrained Bayesian parameters. We therefore report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model. In practice, contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning items), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\sum_r c_{nr} \cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.
2	
3	Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].
4	
```

{

  "new_string": "\\label{tab:bias_absorption}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}\n\\label{app:synthetic_calibration}\n\nWe generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\\tau = 0.954$ vs.\\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\\geq 1{,}678$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Sequential} & \\textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \\textbf{Improv.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\theta$ RMSE & $0.166 \\pm 0.049$ & $0.083 \\pm 0.024$ & $-50.1\\%$ \\\\\n$\\theta$ $\\tau$ & $0.882 \\pm 0.117$ & $0.954 \\pm 0.046$ & $+7.2$pp \\\\\n$c_n$ RMSE & $1.349$ & $0.503$ & $-62.7\\%$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Synthetic calibration at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Joint G-MFRM halves $\\theta$-RMSE and reduces $c_n$-RMSE by 63\\% relative to sequential estimation.}\n\\label{tab:synthetic_calibration}\n\\end{table}"
}

          " The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",

Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected; $c_n$ estimates are best interpreted as directional diagnostics rather than calibrated intervals.",
    "originalFile": "We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, posterior CIs exhibit moderate overconfidence (empirical coverage 71\\% vs.\\ 95\\% nominal; 50 Monte Carlo seeds; \\citealp{talts2018sbc}) due to the HalfNormal boundary constraint on $c_n$, a known limitation of constrained Bayesian parameters. We therefore report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model. In practice, contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning items), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\\sum_r c_{nr} \\cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.\n\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].\n",

          " ",
          " LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$, SE $= 9.4$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.",
          " ",
          "-Ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). However, synthetic replication at matched scale confirms this effect is consistent and statistically significant across 20 independent seeds (Wilcoxon $p < 0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}), suggesting that real-data non-significance reflects single-dataset bootstrap conservatism rather than method failure.",
          "+Ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). Synthetic replication at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$) confirms a significant effect across 20 seeds (Wilcoxon $p < 0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}).",
          " ",
          " The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",
          " "

```text
Page 9 shows:
- Top left: "well-powered validation requires N ≥ 20 (Limitations). Treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly."
- Then "Limitations" starts
- Right column: References

So the Conclusion overflows by about 3 lines onto page 9. I need to save ~3 more lines. The sentence I shortened was helpful but not enough. Let me look at what I can further compress on page 8.

Options:
1. Further shorten the Conclusion


2. Compress §4.3 Error Propagation Robustness
3. Compress Related Work further

The Conclusion is already very tight, so I should explore compressing the error propagation section or trimming Related Work further. Let me see how much space I can reclaim from those sections without losing critical content. I'm validating the theoretical prediction about noise tolerance by injecting Gaussian noise into exposure values and refitting the model across multiple seeds. Looking at the error propagation results, ability and contamination estimates stay relatively stable below σ = 0.2 (degrading by ~6-8%), but degrade sharply beyond that threshold (36-58% increase). Contamination estimates prove more fragile since they depend on the product of exposure and contamination in the likelihood. Importantly, rank ordering remains robust even at higher noise levels.

The ablation study in the appendix reveals that joint estimation substantially outperforms staged estimation—switching to staged fitting doubles the ability RMSE and drops rank correlation by 8 percentage points. Continuous gating also provides a meaningful 40% improvement in contamination recovery over binary gating. Removing the diagnostic component doesn't affect rankings but does degrade contamination recovery by 6%, confirming its utility.
```

The dominant effect is joint versus staged estimation: staged estimation degrades $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($\\tau$ drops 8.3pp, $p < 0.001$), directly validating the OVB analysis; continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary gating ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ does not affect rankings ($\\tau$ identical, $p = 0.34$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$), confirming its role as a diagnostic component.",

          " Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\\% and $+$57.9\\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).",

{
  "parentUuid": "8aa98139-0ddc-4568-a50e-44dc2b0602ae",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 316 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. After filtering for full coverage across all judges and target models, the evaluation set comprises 176 items in a connected incomplete design (5{,}661 observations; 44.7\\% fill rate of the full $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Eight target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Nine LLM judges score all model responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.7, Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Mistral-Large-2, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Position swapping controls for position bias and doubles the effective observation count. Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs systematic self-evaluation bias, and excluding self-judging observations leaves rankings unchanged ($\\tau = 0.857$; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated via a two-source pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}): reference model anchoring with gpt-4-0314 as anchor (75\\% weight) and cross-model agreement (25\\% weight). The pipeline is robust to weight configuration (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.998$ across all configurations tested).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} We use NumPyro NUTS with 4{,}000 warmup and 4{,}000 sampling iterations across 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). The contamination prior follows the hierarchical HalfNormal specification (Eq.~\\ref{eq:contam_prior}). Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} with conventional $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS $> 100$ thresholds \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines and Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven methods that address judge bias or contamination separately: (1) Raw Average, (2) MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), (3) Sequential pipeline (MFRM then contamination adjustment), (4) CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, (5) CyclicJudge+discard (exposed items removed), (6) DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and (7) Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\tau$ & $\\rho$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nRaw Average & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nMFRM-only & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nSequential & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nCyclicJudge+discard & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nDCR Factor & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\nPref.\\ Leakage & 0.786 & 0.905 \\\\\n\\midrule\nG-MFRM (ours) & \\textbf{0.857} & \\textbf{0.952} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Correlation with Chatbot Arena human rankings on Arena-Hard (8 target models, 9 judges). All seven baselines produce identical rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$). G-MFRM yields nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($+$7.1pp; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll seven baselines produce identical model rankings ($\\tau = 0.786$, $\\rho = 0.905$). With $N = 8$, Kendall $\\tau$ takes only ${\\sim}20$ distinct values, but raw ability scores also vary by $< 0.03$ logits across methods (Table~\\ref{tab:raw_scores}), confirming genuine invariance rather than discretization artifact. This reflects the structural confounding from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}: single-facet corrections leave the uncorrected confound intact (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). That CyclicJudge+discard also preserves rankings suggests contamination effects on aggregate rankings are modest at this scale; G-MFRM's primary value lies in per-model contamination diagnostics. G-MFRM achieves nominally higher point-estimate rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$, $\\rho = 0.952$) over the baselines ($\\tau = 0.786$), but the posterior median $\\tau$ equals the baseline (0.786), with the point estimate at the distribution's upper tail. Bootstrap resampling (4{,}000 posterior draws) yields $\\Delta\\tau$ 95\\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$: the ranking improvement is not statistically significant at $N = 8$, though G-MFRM almost never degrades rankings ($P(\\Delta\\tau \\geq 0) = 0.927$). On synthetic data ($N = 50$), joint estimation significantly outperforms sequential ($\\tau = 0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$), confirming that the effect is real but below detection at this sample size. The rank change---gpt-4o-mini and qwen2.5-72b swap positions---is consistent with differential contamination ($P(\\theta_{\\text{mini}} > \\theta_{\\text{qwen}}) = 0.853$).\n\nWe evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.\n\nLOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -60.0$, SE $= 9.4$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.\n\nRanking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). Synthetic replication at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$) confirms a significant effect across 20 seeds (Wilcoxon $p < 0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}).\n\nThe MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:error_prop} predicted that ability estimates degrade gracefully when exposure noise $\\sigma$ remains below $\\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)} \\approx 0.2$. We validate this prediction by injecting Gaussian noise into the true exposure values and re-fitting G-MFRM on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds; exp-012).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\sigma$ & $\\theta$-RMSE & $\\Delta\\theta$\\% & $c_n$-RMSE & $\\Delta c_n$\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.0 & 0.128 $\\pm$ 0.018 & --- & 0.533 $\\pm$ 0.187 & --- \\\\\n0.1 & 0.130 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +1.4 & 0.545 $\\pm$ 0.192 & +2.3 \\\\\n0.2 & 0.135 $\\pm$ 0.018 & +5.9 & 0.575 $\\pm$ 0.198 & +7.9 \\\\\n0.5 & 0.175 $\\pm$ 0.027 & +36.9 & 0.842 $\\pm$ 0.281 & +57.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Error propagation robustness (exp-012). Ability RMSE degrades by less than 6\\% when exposure noise $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, matching the theoretical prediction. Contamination estimates are more sensitive due to multiplicative coupling with $e_i$.}\n\\label{tab:error_prop}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} confirms this prediction: below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial ($+$36.9\\% and $+$57.9\\%). Contamination estimates are more sensitive because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$. Rank ordering is preserved even at $\\sigma = 0.5$ ($\\tau > 0.88$).\n\nAblation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation increases $\\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\\times$ ($p < 0.001$); continuous gating improves $c_n$-RMSE by 40\\% over binary ($p < 0.001$). Removing $d_j$ preserves rankings but degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6\\%, $p < 0.001$).\n",
        "numLines": 77,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 77
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
        "content": "We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage differs by parameter type: ability estimates $\\theta$ approach nominal coverage at moderate sample sizes (${\\sim}$90\\% at $N = 8$), while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibits moderate posterior overconfidence (71\\% at $N = 50$, 58\\% at $N = 8$; \\citealp{talts2018sbc}). The $c_n$ under-coverage reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$, which systematically narrows posterior credible intervals. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected; $c_n$ estimates are best interpreted as directional diagnostics rather than calibrated intervals. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model. In practice, contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning items), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\\sum_r c_{nr} \\cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.\n\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nCyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias through round-robin judge assignment but treats residual score variation as genuine ability. UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. \\citet{ye2024justice} and \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} further document position, verbosity, and self-favoritism biases. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection and Adjustment.}\nSeveral methods detect or adjust for benchmark contamination---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}, Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024}---but all assume a fixed rater, so judge severity can masquerade as contamination. \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side contamination (same-family leakage), complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Avoidance.}\nLiveBench \\citep{livebench2025} and TRUCE \\citep{truce2024} prevent contamination by design through frequently updated items and confidential computing, respectively. tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} reduce cost via IRT-based item selection. These approaches are complementary: avoidance reduces exposed items, while G-MFRM corrects for residual contamination.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\n\\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to LLM raters without contamination terms; \\citet{uto2020generalized}, \\citet{choi2026irt_judge}, and PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extend IRT/MFRM along other axes but none include contamination facets. The closest antecedent, DG-IRT \\citep{shu2013dgirt}, is compared in Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}.",

  "isSidechain": false,

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (estimated $c_n > 1.0$ for top-4 models, directionally consistent with known training data exposure; posterior CI coverage is 71\\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -60$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 6.4$).\n\\end{abstract}",
        "numLines": 3,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 3
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

_source line 3721 · 2026-05-23T10:00:11.047Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] exp-017 早期 coverage 数据 — 不等实验完成，先 patch 已有数据

**已有数据** (exp-017 N=8, 16 seeds + exp-009 N=50, 50 seeds):
- N=8: θ coverage = 89.8%, c_n coverage = 58.3%
- N=50 (exp-009): c_n coverage = 71%

**Limitations 第四点已更新过 θ/c_n 分离**。现在在 Appendix 新增一小段 "CI Coverage Analysis" (app:ci_coverage)：

"We assess empirical coverage of 95\% posterior credible intervals across synthetic replications. At $N=8$ (16 seeds), ability parameter $\theta$ achieves 89.8\% coverage, approaching the nominal level, while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ achieves only 58.3\%. At $N=50$ (50 seeds; exp-009), $c_n$ coverage improves to 71\%. The coverage gap between $\theta$ and $c_n$ reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \cdot e_i$: contamination parameters enter the likelihood through an interaction term, making posterior inference more sensitive to exposure estimation noise and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$. These results support treating $c_n$ point estimates and rank orderings as reliable diagnostics, while noting that $c_n$ credible intervals are systematically too narrow."

**注意**: 这段使用的是 exp-017 的 16-seed 早期数据。如果 exp-017 完成后数字有变（200 seeds），会再 patch 更新。当前先用 16 seeds 的值。

**编译确认 8 页正文。**

```text
1	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}}
2	\label{app:proof}
3	
4	We prove that the G-MFRM is locally identifiable under the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}. Following \citet{rothenberg1971}, a parametric model is locally identifiable at the true parameter vector $\boldsymbol{\psi}_0$ if and only if the Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_0)$ is non-singular.
5	
6	\paragraph{Setup.} The G-MFRM parameter vector is $\boldsymbol{\psi} = (\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$, where $\boldsymbol{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^{N-1}$ (after the location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$), $\mathbf{c} \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^N$, $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^I$, $\mathbf{d} \in \mathbb{R}^J$, and $\boldsymbol{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^{K-1}$. The exposure vector $\mathbf{e} = (e_1, \ldots, e_I)$ is fixed externally and treated as known. The linear predictor is $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$, with partial derivatives:
7	%
8	\begin{align*}
9	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \theta_n} &= 1, \quad
10	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial c_n} = e_i, \quad
11	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial b_i} = -1, \\
12	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial d_j} &= -1, \quad
13	\frac{\partial \eta_{nijk}}{\partial \tau_k} = -1.
14	\end{align*}
15	
16	\paragraph{Fisher information weights.} For each observation cell $(n,i,j)$, the Fisher information weight under the cumulative logit model is
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:fisher_weight}
20	w_{nij} = \sum_{k=1}^{K} \frac{\bigl[\sigma'(\eta_{nijk}) - \sigma'(\eta_{nij,k+1})\bigr]^2}{P(Y_{nij} = k)},
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where $\sigma(x) = (1 + e^{-x})^{-1}$, $\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1 - \sigma(x))$, and we set $\sigma'(\eta_{nij,0}) = \sigma'(\eta_{nij,K+1}) = 0$ at the boundaries. Since $P(Y_{nij} = k) > 0$ for all categories and $\sigma'(\cdot) > 0$, we have $w_{nij} > 0$ for all $(n,i,j)$.
24	
25	\paragraph{Step 1: The $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.} For each model $n$, the $2 \times 2$ Fisher information submatrix is
26	%
27	\begin{equation}
28	\label{eq:fisher_block}
29	\mathbf{F}_n = \sum_{i=1}^{I} \sum_{j=1}^{J} w_{nij}
30	\begin{pmatrix} 1 & e_i \\ e_i & e_i^2 \end{pmatrix}.
31	\end{equation}
32	%
33	Its determinant is
34	%
35	\begin{multline}
36	\det \mathbf{F}_n = \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\Bigr)\Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i^2\Bigr) \\
37	- \Bigl(\textstyle\sum_{i,j} w_{nij}\, e_i\Bigr)^2 = W_n^2 \cdot \mathrm{Var}_w(e_i),
38	\end{multline}
39	%
40	where $W_n = \sum_{i,j} w_{nij}$ and $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \overline{e^2}_n - \bar{e}_n^2$ is the weighted variance of exposure with weights $w_{nij}$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) \geq 0$, with equality if and only if $e_i$ is constant across all items with $w_{nij} > 0$. Under conditions~(i) and~(iii), the observed items for model $n$ include at least two distinct exposure values with $w_{nij} > 0$ for the corresponding cells. Therefore $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) > 0$, $\det \mathbf{F}_n > 0$, and each $\mathbf{F}_n$ is positive definite.
41	
42	\paragraph{Step 2: Item and judge blocks.} Under the connected design (condition~i), each item $i$ is observed by at least one $(n,j)$ pair, so the item difficulty block has positive diagonal entries $[\mathbf{F}_{bb}]_{ii} = \sum_{n,j} w_{nij} > 0$ (summing over observed cells). Similarly, each judge $j$ observes at least one $(n,i)$ pair, giving $[\mathbf{F}_{dd}]_{jj} = \sum_{n,i} w_{nij} > 0$. Condition~(ii) ($J \geq 2$) ensures the judge block has sufficient dimension to separate judge effects from the intercept. Both blocks are positive definite.
43	
44	\paragraph{Step 3: Schur complement argument.} The full Fisher information matrix $\mathbf{F}$ contains off-diagonal blocks coupling the parameter groups. Partition as
45	%
46	\begin{equation*}
47	\mathbf{F} = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf{A} & \mathbf{B} \\ \mathbf{B}^\top & \mathbf{D} \end{pmatrix},
48	\end{equation*}
49	%
50	where $\mathbf{A} = \mathrm{blockdiag}(\mathbf{F}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{F}_N)$ collects all $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrices and $\mathbf{D}$ collects the $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ blocks. From Step~1, $\mathbf{A}$ is positive definite. The Schur complement
51	%
52	\begin{equation}
53	\label{eq:schur}
54	\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{D} - \mathbf{B}^\top \mathbf{A}^{-1} \mathbf{B}
55	\end{equation}
56	%
57	equals the conditional Fisher information for $(\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ given $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$. Conditioning on $(\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c})$ with fixed $\mathbf{e}$ reduces the model to a standard MFRM with known offsets $c_n \cdot e_i$, whose identifiability is established by \citet[Chapter~5]{sanmartin2016} under conditions~(i), (ii), and~(iv). Therefore $\mathbf{S}$ is positive definite.
58	
59	Since both $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{S}$ are positive definite:
60	%
61	\begin{equation*}
62	\det \mathbf{F} = \det \mathbf{A} \cdot \det \mathbf{S} > 0,
63	\end{equation*}
64	%
65	establishing non-singularity of $\mathbf{F}$ and hence local identifiability. \qed
66	
67	\paragraph{Local vs.\ global identifiability.} Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} establishes \emph{local} identifiability: the Fisher information matrix is non-singular at the true parameter value, guaranteeing that no infinitesimally close parameter vector produces the same likelihood. This does not rule out discrete global non-identifiabilities (i.e., isolated points in parameter space with identical likelihood). However, the HalfNormal prior on $c_n$ restricts the parameter space to $c_n \geq 0$, eliminating the sign ambiguity that would otherwise create a global reflection $(\theta_n + \delta,\, c_n - \delta/e_i)$. Combined with the location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$ and the empirical observation that the posterior is unimodal across all chains (no mode-switching detected), the local result provides a practical guarantee of unique parameter recovery.
68	
69	\paragraph{Role of the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.} The key step in Step~1 is the decomposition $\det \mathbf{F}_n = W_n^2 \cdot \mathrm{Var}_w(e_i)$, where the weighted variance $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = \mathbb{E}_w[e_i^2] - (\mathbb{E}_w[e_i])^2 \geq 0$ by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to the vectors $(\sqrt{w_{nij}})$ and $(\sqrt{w_{nij}}\, e_i)$. Strict positivity follows from the requirement that $e_i$ takes at least two distinct values over observed items (condition~iii): if $e_i = e$ for all $i$ with $w_{nij} > 0$, then $\mathrm{Var}_w(e_i) = 0$ and the $(\theta_n, c_n)$ block becomes singular, recovering Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}. The Cauchy--Schwarz bound is tight only in this degenerate case, so any non-trivial exposure variation suffices for identification.
70	
71	
72	\section{Explicit Form of the OVB Coefficient $\gamma_n$}
73	\label{app:gamma}
74	
75	Under the MFRM logistic link, a first-order Taylor expansion of Observation~\ref{obs:ovb} yields an explicit approximation for the bias coefficient $\gamma_n$:
76	%
77	\begin{equation}
78	\label{eq:gamma_explicit}
79	\gamma_n \approx \frac{\sum_{i} w_{ni} \cdot e_i \cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})}{\sum_{i} w_{ni} \cdot p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})},
80	\end{equation}
81	%
82	where $p_{ni} = \sigma(\theta_n - b_i - \bar{d})$ is the MFRM predicted probability averaged over judges and $w_{ni}$ is the effective item weight (number of judge observations for item $i$ on model $n$). Eq.~\ref{eq:gamma_explicit} is a weighted mean of the exposure values $e_i$, with weights proportional to the Fisher information $p_{ni}(1 - p_{ni})$. Items near the boundary ($p_{ni} \approx 0.5$) contribute more to the bias than items at the extremes, because the logistic link is most sensitive at these points. This approximation is accurate to first order in $(c_n \cdot e_i)$; the synthetic validation in Section~\ref{sec:confounding} ($r = 0.86$) confirms that the linear approximation captures the dominant bias structure.
83	
84	
85	\section{Murphy--Topel Variance Correction}
86	\label{app:murphy_topel}
87	
88	The three-stage G-MFRM procedure treats Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed in Stage~3, understating posterior uncertainty by ignoring estimation error in $\hat{e}_i$. We derive the Murphy--Topel correction \citep{murphy1985} adapted to the G-MFRM setting.
89	
90	\paragraph{Setup.} Let $\boldsymbol{\alpha} = (e_1, \ldots, e_I)$ denote the Stage~2 parameters estimated from an independent dataset with log-likelihood $\ell_2(\boldsymbol{\alpha})$, and let $\boldsymbol{\psi} = (\boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{d}, \boldsymbol{\tau})$ denote the Stage~3 parameters estimated with log-likelihood $\ell_3(\boldsymbol{\psi}; \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})$, where $\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}$ is plugged in as known.
91	
92	\paragraph{Naive and Stage~2 variances.} The naive (uncorrected) variance of $\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}$ is
93	%
94	\begin{equation}
95	\mathbf{V}_3 = \left[-\frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial \boldsymbol{\psi}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\psi}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\, \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right]^{-1},
96	\end{equation}
97	%
98	and the Stage~2 variance is
99	%
100	\begin{equation}
101	\mathbf{V}_2 = \left[-\frac{\partial^2 \ell_2}{\partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right]^{-1}.
102	\end{equation}
103	
104	\paragraph{Cross-derivative matrix.} The coupling between stages is captured by the cross-derivative
105	%
106	\begin{equation}
107	\mathbf{C} = \frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial \boldsymbol{\psi}\, \partial \boldsymbol{\alpha}^\top}\bigg|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}},\, \hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}.
108	\end{equation}
109	%
110	In the G-MFRM, non-zero entries of $\mathbf{C}$ arise from the contamination gating term $c_n \cdot e_i$. The key entry is
111	%
112	\begin{equation}
113	\frac{\partial^2 \ell_3}{\partial c_n\, \partial e_i} = \sum_{j=1}^{J} \frac{\partial^2 \ell_{nij}}{\partial \eta_{nij}^2} \cdot c_n,
114	\end{equation}
115	%
116	which is proportional to $c_n$: models with zero contamination contribute no cross-derivative. Similarly, $\partial^2 \ell_3 / (\partial \theta_n\, \partial e_i)$ involves only indirect coupling through the shared item observations. The correction is therefore concentrated on the contamination parameters of models with $c_n > 0$.
117	
118	\paragraph{Corrected variance.} The Murphy--Topel corrected variance is
119	%
120	\begin{equation}
121	\label{eq:murphy_topel}
122	\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}} = \mathbf{V}_3 + \mathbf{V}_3\, \mathbf{C}\, \mathbf{V}_2\, \mathbf{C}^\top\, \mathbf{V}_3.
123	\end{equation}
124	%
125	The correction term $\mathbf{V}_3 \mathbf{C} \mathbf{V}_2 \mathbf{C}^\top \mathbf{V}_3$ is positive semi-definite, so $\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}} \succeq \mathbf{V}_3$: the corrected variance is always at least as large as the naive variance. The inflation is concentrated on $c_n$ for contaminated models and is negligible for $\theta_n$ when contamination is sparse, consistent with the empirical pattern in Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}.
126	
127	\paragraph{Implementation.} We estimate $\mathbf{V}_2$ from the Stage~2 bootstrap and compute $\mathbf{C}$ via finite differences of the Stage~3 log-likelihood evaluated at perturbed $\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}$ values. Corrected credible intervals are obtained by inflating Stage~3 posterior samples by $\sqrt{\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{MT}}) / \mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{V}_3)}$.
128	
129	
130	\section{Ablation Study}
131	\label{app:ablation}
132	
133	\begin{table}[h]
134	\centering
135	\scriptsize
136	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
137	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
138	\toprule
139	\textbf{Variant} & $\theta$-RMSE & $\tau$ & $c_n$-RMSE \\
140	\midrule
141	Full G-MFRM & 0.124 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.593 $\pm$ 0.224 \\
142	$-$ Judge severity ($d_j$) & 0.127 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.922 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.629 $\pm$ 0.243 \\
143	Binary gating & 0.137 $\pm$ 0.016 & 0.909 $\pm$ 0.014 & 0.833 $\pm$ 0.226 \\
144	Staged estimation & 0.254 $\pm$ 0.045 & 0.839 $\pm$ 0.035 & 1.382 $\pm$ 0.164 \\
145	\bottomrule
146	\end{tabular}
147	\caption{Ablation study on synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds). Each row removes or modifies one component of the full G-MFRM. Staged estimation refers to fitting MFRM and contamination sequentially rather than jointly. All differences from Full G-MFRM are significant at $p < 0.001$ (Wilcoxon) except $-d_j$ on $\theta$-RMSE ($p = 0.245$) and $\tau$ ($p = 0.344$).}
148	\label{tab:ablation}
149	\end{table}
150	
151	The ablation confirms that joint estimation is the primary contributor to G-MFRM's advantage: staged estimation increases $\theta$-RMSE by $2.0\times$ (from 0.124 to 0.254, $p < 0.001$) and $c_n$-RMSE by $2.3\times$ (from 0.593 to 1.382, $p < 0.001$), with $\tau$ dropping from 0.922 to 0.839. This degradation directly reflects the omitted variable bias characterized in Section~\ref{sec:confounding}: when contamination and judge effects are estimated sequentially, the first-stage estimates absorb the unmodeled facet.
152	
153	Replacing continuous exposure $e_i \in [0,1]$ with binary gating ($e_i \in \{0, 1\}$) increases $c_n$-RMSE by 40\% (from 0.593 to 0.833, $p < 0.001$), indicating that graded exposure modeling captures contamination magnitude more precisely than a binary flag. The effect on $\theta$-RMSE is moderate ($+$10\%, $p < 0.001$), suggesting that the binary approximation still captures the dominant contamination signal but loses precision on partially exposed items.
154	
155	Removing judge severity $d_j$ does not significantly affect ability estimates ($\theta$-RMSE $+$2.4\%, $p = 0.245$; $\tau$ identical, $p = 0.344$) but significantly degrades contamination recovery ($c_n$-RMSE $+$6.1\%, $p < 0.001$). This confirms that $d_j$ serves as a diagnostic component that improves contamination estimation precision rather than ranking accuracy: without explicit judge severity modeling, judge-induced score variation is partially absorbed into the contamination estimates, inflating $c_n$-RMSE. The $d_j$ profile also provides independent diagnostic value for judge calibration (e.g., Claude Opus $d_j = +0.60$ vs.\ Mistral $-0.74$ in the real-data evaluation).
156	
157	
158	\section{Posterior Predictive Check}
159	\label{app:ppc}
160	
161	Figure~\ref{fig:ppc} shows the posterior predictive distribution from 1{,}000 posterior draws over 5{,}661 observations. The predicted and observed response distributions are well-aligned (predicted mean win rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$), with no systematic discrepancy across item difficulty or model ability levels.
162	
163	\begin{figure}[h]
164	\centering
165	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ppc}
166	\caption{Posterior predictive check. Distribution of predicted (gray) and observed (black) response rates across all model--item--judge triplets. Mean predicted $= 0.593$, mean observed $= 0.599$.}
167	\label{fig:ppc}
168	\end{figure}
169	
170	
171	\section{OVB Validation Across Confounding Levels}
172	\label{app:ovb_validation}
173	
174	\begin{figure}[h]

176	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_bias_vs_rho}
177	\caption{Sequential estimator bias remains approximately constant across confounding levels ($\rho$), confirming omission-driven rather than confounding-strength-dependent bias. Joint G-MFRM estimation reduces bias by approximately 70\% at all $\rho$ levels. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 50 seeds.}
178	\label{fig:bias_vs_rho}
179	\end{figure}
180	
181	
182	\section{Contamination Susceptibility Recovery}
183	\label{app:cn_recovery}
184	
185	\begin{figure}[h]
186	\centering
187	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_cn_recovery}
188	\caption{Contamination susceptibility recovery ($c_n$: true vs.\ estimated) from 50 Monte Carlo seeds. Empirical CI coverage is 71\% (below nominal 95\%), indicating moderate posterior overconfidence (see Limitations).}
189	\label{fig:cn_recovery}
190	\end{figure}
191	
192	
193	\section{Error Propagation Figure}
194	\label{app:error_prop_fig}
195	
196	\begin{figure}[h]
197	\centering
198	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_error_propagation}
199	\caption{Error propagation from Stage~2 exposure noise to Stage~3 parameter estimates. (a)~Ability RMSE ($\theta$) and (b)~contamination RMSE ($c_n$) as a function of exposure noise $\sigma_e$. The dashed vertical line marks $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}} \approx 0.2$, below which degradation remains mild ($\theta$-RMSE $+$5.9\%, $c_n$-RMSE $+$7.9\%). Error bars show $\pm$1 std over 20 seeds. See Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} for numerical values.}
200	\label{fig:error_prop}
201	\end{figure}
202	
203	
204	\section{Self-Evaluation Robustness}
205	\label{app:self_eval}
206	
207	Two models---Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B---appear as both judge and target in our evaluation. Self-judging introduces a potential confound: a model may score its own responses more favorably. We examine the empirical magnitude of this effect and its impact on rankings.
208	
209	Qwen2.5-72B achieves a perfect self-judging win rate of 1.000 (42/42 items), compared to a cross-judge mean win rate of 0.714 for the same model. LLaMA-3.3-70B shows a self-judging win rate of 0.912, compared to a cross-judge mean of 0.823. Both values are elevated relative to cross-judge baselines, consistent with same-family preference leakage \citep{preference_leakage2026}.
210	
211	To assess sensitivity, we re-fit G-MFRM excluding all 99 self-judging observations (Qwen2.5-72B and LLaMA-3.3-70B judging their own responses). The resulting ranking yields $\tau = 0.857$, identical to the full-data G-MFRM point estimate, with a swap at ranks 2--3. Contamination susceptibility estimates $c_n$ shift by less than 0.15 for all models. This confirms that $d_j$ absorbs the bulk of self-evaluation bias and that rankings are robust to excluding self-judging pairs.
212	
213	
214	\section{Judge Severity Profiles}
215	\label{app:judge_severity}
216	
217	Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity} reports posterior estimates of judge severity $d_j$ for all nine judges. Positive values indicate stricter scoring (lower win rates); negative values indicate more lenient scoring. Proprietary models cluster at the strict end, while open-source models tend to be more lenient.

241	
242	\section{Judge Count Sensitivity}
243	\label{app:judge_count}
244	
245	Table~\ref{tab:judge_count} reports ranking accuracy as a function of the number of judges $k$, obtained by subsampling from the full 9-judge panel (10 random subsets per $k$; $k = 9$ uses the full panel). G-MFRM outperforms both raw averaging and sequential estimation at all judge counts. Rank agreement increases monotonically with $k$ ($\tau = 0.771 \to 0.857$), and variance contracts as additional judges improve $d_j$ and $c_n$ estimation. At $k \geq 4$, G-MFRM exceeds the full-panel baseline $\tau = 0.786$, suggesting that four diverse judges suffice for practical deployment.
246	
247	\begin{table}[h]
248	\centering
249	\small
250	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
251	\toprule
252	$k$ & Raw Avg & Sequential & G-MFRM & $\Delta\tau$ \\
253	\midrule
254	2 & $.755 \pm .088$ & $.764 \pm .096$ & $.771 \pm .081$ & $+.017$ \\
255	4 & $.779 \pm .041$ & $.786 \pm .048$ & $.800 \pm .045$ & $+.021$ \\
256	6 & $.800 \pm .030$ & $.807 \pm .048$ & $.821 \pm .051$ & $+.021$ \\
257	9 & $.786$ & $.786$ & $.857$ & $+.071$ \\
258	\bottomrule
259	\end{tabular}
260	\caption{Kendall $\tau$ with Chatbot Arena rankings as a function of judge count $k$. $\Delta\tau$ = G-MFRM $-$ Raw Average. Values for $k < 9$ are mean $\pm$ std over 10 random judge subsets.}
261	\label{tab:judge_count}
262	\end{table}
263	
264	Leave-one-judge-out analysis reveals that three judges---Claude Opus 4.7 ($d_j = +0.60$), GPT-5.5 ($+0.53$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($-0.42$)---are individually necessary for the ranking improvement: removing any one of these reduces G-MFRM $\tau$ to the baseline ($0.786$), while removing any of the other six judges preserves $\tau = 0.857$. These three judges span the severity spectrum (the two strictest and the second-most lenient), suggesting that contamination$\times$judge interaction identification requires sufficient severity diversity. This provides practical guidance: judge panels should include models with diverse scoring tendencies rather than homogeneous judges.
265	
266	G-MFRM requires $\geq 4$ MCMC chains for reliable contamination estimation; reduced chain counts attenuate $c_n$ estimates toward the prior mean, compressing inter-model differences.
267

290	
291	
292	\section{Power Analysis}
293	\label{app:power}
294	
295	The observed effect size in the real-data evaluation is $\Delta\tau = 0.071$ (G-MFRM $\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ baseline $\tau = 0.786$). We conduct a permutation-based power analysis to determine the sample sizes required to detect this effect at $\alpha = 0.05$.
296	
297	We simulate ranking comparisons under the alternative hypothesis $H_1\!: \Delta\tau = 0.071$ by bootstrapping from the observed posterior distribution of G-MFRM ability estimates and recomputing $\tau$ against ground truth at varying model counts $N$. For each $N$, we draw 10{,}000 permutation replicates and compute the rejection rate at $\alpha = 0.05$ (two-sided).
298	
299	At $N = 8$ (our evaluation), power is approximately 50\%, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result ($\Delta\tau$ 95\% CI $[-0.071, 0.071]$). Achieving 80\% power requires $N \approx 17$ models, and 95\% power requires $N \approx 25$. These estimates inform the scope of future evaluations: detecting ranking improvements of the magnitude observed here requires at minimum doubling the number of target models.
300

331	\label{app:bias_absorption}
332

The difference $\Delta\theta = \hat{\theta}_{\text{MFRM}} - \hat{\theta}_{\text{G-MFRM}}$ is perfectly rank-correlated with contamination susceptibility $c_n$ (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$), providing direct empirical evidence that MFRM-only absorbs the omitted contamination term into ability estimates.

358	\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}
359	\label{app:synthetic_calibration}
360	
361	We generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\tau = 0.954$ vs.\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\Delta\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\geq 1{,}678$).
